# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: King Augustus III (reigned 1734–1763) as King of Poland and Grand Duke of Lithuania.

[^2]: Sylvester Lubieniecki-Rudnytsky (1700–1777), Basilian Bishop of Lutsk and Ostroh (1750–1777), named Administrator and Exarch of the Kyivan Metropolia.

[^3]: Hypatius Bilynsky, OSBM (1704–1771), Protoarchimandrite of the Ruthenian Order of Saint Basil the Great (Congregation of the Holy Trinity) from 1751 to 1771.

[^4]: The dating formula gives the Byzantine Anno Mundi year 7269 (*з҃сѯѳ҃*) and the Anno Domini year 1761 (*а҃ѱѯа*).

[^5]: The Synaxis of the Most Holy Theotokos at the Monastery of Miasena (*in Miasenis*) commemorates the miraculous preservation and recovery of her icon cast into Lake Zagoura during the iconoclastic persecution.

[^6]: An allusion to LXX Psalm 140:7: "As when a thick clump of earth is broken upon the earth, so have our bones been scattered beside Hades."

[^7]: Isaiah 61:1–9 (LXX). Prescribed liturgical reading for the New Church Year (Indiction).

[^8]: Leviticus 26:3–12, 14–24 (LXX). Prescribed reading of blessings and curses for the Indiction.

[^9]: Wisdom of Solomon 4:7–15. Common reading for venerable monastic saints.

[^10]: Physical Leaf p8 (Fol. 3v).

[^11]: The acrostic in Slavonic reads: *Твердыхъ женъ мнцъ: пою болѣзни* ("I sing the pangs of the steadfast women martyrs"). In the Greek Menaion, this canon is composed by St. Theophanes: *Στερρῶν γυναικῶν ἆθλα μέλπω μαρτύρων*.

[^12]: The Katavasiae of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross (*Cross, inscribed by Moses*) are appointed to be chanted from September 1 through the Apodosis of the Exaltation on September 21.

[^13]: The rubric indicates that this Katavasia is chanted daily throughout the festive cycle ending on the Apodosis of the Precious Cross (*ѿда́нїе чтⷭ҇на́ крⷭ҇та̀*), September 21.

[^14]: Slavonic: *правосла́внаго короля̀* ("the Orthodox King"), standard titulature in Pochaiv Lavra printings under the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth referring to the ruling Crown of Poland (King Augustus III, r. 1733–1763).

[^15]: An allusion to the traditional hagiographical account of St. Symeon the Stylite, who according to his Life by Theodoret of Cyrrhus and Antonius spent the entire forty days of Great Lent without tasting any food or water, receiving only the Holy Mysteries on Pascha or at the midpoint of the Fast.

[^16]: This rubrical directive specifies that the Biblical Canticle of the Theotokos (*Magnificat* / *My soul doth magnify the Lord*) with its refrain *More honorable than the Cherubim* is sung at the 9th Ode unless the day is a Feast of the Lord, even on days with the Great Doxology or the patronal feast of a temple saint.

[^17]: *preserve in peace the king (королѧ̀)*: The Pochaiv Menaion, printed in 1761 within the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth under royal patronage (King Augustus III, r. 1734–1763, named on the title leaf p1), explicitly petitions for the reigning Polish monarch under the vernacular East Slavic/Polish title *король*. This recensional reading contrasts sharply with contemporary Moscow synodal editions, which petition for the Russian Emperor (*цр҃ѧ́* / *царя*), and traditional Byzantine Greek editions, which preserve the imperial plural (*τοὺς βασιλεῖς*).

[^18]: *By Andrew of Pyrgos (А҆ндре́а пѵ́рскагѡ)*: St. Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), Archbishop of Gortyna, who is frequently designated in Greek hymnographic manuscripts and Slavonic liturgical codices by his monastic origin or epithet (variously *τοῦ Πυρροῦ*, *Andrew of Pyrgos*, or *Andrew of Jerusalem*).

[^19]: *One should know (Подоба́етъ вѣ́дати)*: This rubrical chapter sets forth the liturgical order when September 1 (the Indiction) coincides with a Sunday, prescribing the omission of the office of the forty holy ascetic women and detailing the combined Resurrectional and festal sequence at Vespers, Matins, and Divine Liturgy.

[^20]: *Memory of the Holy Martyr Mamas (Ст҃а́гѡ мч҃ника ма́манта)*: Saint Mamas of Caesarea in Cappadocia (ca. 260–275), child martyr under Emperor Aurelian, born in prison to martyred Christian parents Theodotus and Rufina, renowned as an ascetic hermit of Mount Argaios.

[^21]: *By Byzantios (Вѷзанті́ево)*: Ascribed in Byzantine liturgical sources to Leo the Despot or other Constantinopolitan hymnographers bearing the appellation *Βυζάντιος* (*the Byzantine*).

[^22]: *The composition of Theophanes (Творе́нїе ѳеофа́ново)*: Saint Theophanes the Branded (Graptos, ca. 778–845), monk of Saint Sabas Monastery, Metropolitan of Nicaea, prominent confessor during the second Iconoclast persecution, and prolific Melodist.

[^23]: *raise the irrational brute creation unto rational hymnody*: An allusion to the hagiographical tradition of Saint Mamas living in ascetic solitude upon Mount Argaios, where wild animals assembled meekly around him, fed from his hand, and were milked by him to provide nourishment for the poor.

[^24]: *a voice from heaven... lightening the pain of thy sufferings*: According to the traditional *Martyrium* and Byzantine Synaxarion of Saint Mamas, while the young martyr was being scraped with iron claws, a celestial voice resounded: *"Be strong and of good courage, Mamas!"*, which miraculously alleviated the physical pain of his wounds.

[^25]: *a most fair dove bearing the food of incorruption*: An allusion to the hagiographical account wherein a white dove visited the imprisoned martyr in his dungeon cell, bearing heavenly nourishment to sustain him during his confinement.

[^26]: *Month of September: Of the Holy Hieromartyr Anthimus... (Мцⷭъ Септе́мврїй ст҃а́гѡ сщ҃нному́ченика а҆нѳі́ма...)*: The right margin of fol. 15v (leaf p32) displays a vertical cinnabar rubric serving as an epigraphic running indicator announcing the commemoration for the following day (September 3).

[^27]: *The Holy Hieromartyr Anthimus... and our Venerable Father Theoctistus (Ст҃а́гѡ сщ҃енному́ченика а҆нѳі́ма... И҆ преподо́бнагѡ ѻ҆ц҃а̀ на́шегѡ ѳеокти́ста)*: Saint Anthimus, Bishop of Nicomedia, was beheaded ca. 303–304 during the Great Persecution under Diocletian and Maximian, following the burning of the 20,000 martyrs in the church of Nicomedia. Saint Theoctistus (d. 451/467) was the companion and co-laborer of Saint Euthymius the Great in the Judean wilderness, establishing the renowned coenobitic lavra in the wadi known as the Monastery of Saint Theoctistus (Deir Mukallik).

[^28]: *Podoben: "On the Third Day" (Тридне́венъ)*: Byzantine model melody (*automelon*) *Τριήμερος ἀνέστης*, characteristic of Tone 6 Stavrotheotokia appointed on Wednesday and Friday cycles in the Octoechos and Menaion.

[^29]: *Canon of the Hieromartyr, whose Acrostic is (Кано́нъ сщ҃енному́ченика, є҆гѡ́же краегране́сїе)*: The acrostic (Slavonic: *краегранесіе*) is an alphabetical/thematic phrase woven through the opening letters of the troparia across the odes. Here the Slavonic reads: *Цвѣ́тъ тѧ̀ пою̀ па́стырей и҆ му́ченикѡвъ* ("The bloom of pastors and martyrs I sing"), reflecting the Greek hymnographic original *Ἀνθῶ σε τὸν ἔξαρχον ποιμένων, ἄθλων*, wordplay on the name of the Hieromartyr *Anthimus* (from Greek *ἄνθος*, bloom, flower).

[^30]: *Thy struggles, O Theoctistus, I recount. By Theophanes (Твоѧ̑ по́двиги ѽ ѳеокті́сте сказу́ю. Деофа́ново)*: The acrostic of the second canon explicitly names its author, Saint Theophanes the Branded (Graptos, ca. 778–845). In the Greek Menaion, the acrostic reads: *Τὰ σὰ παλαίσματα, Θεόκτιστε, φράζω. Θεοφάνους*.

[^31]: *Ode 4. Heirmos: Thou didst mount upon horses... (Сѣ́лъ є҆сѝ на ко́ни...)*: An allusive liturgical citation of the Prophet Habakkuk's canticle: 'Thou didst mount upon Thine horses, and Thy chariots were salvation' (Habakkuk 3:8).

[^32]: *In the tomb a saint buried thy body, one who was bright namesake of good cheer (Во гро́бѣ тѣ́ло твоѐ ст҃ъ погребѐ, и҆ бл҃году́шїѧ свѣ́тлый тезоимени́тъ)*: An explicit allusion to Saint Euthymius the Great, whose Greek name *Εὐθύμιος* derives from *εὔθυμος* ('of good cheer', 'encouraging', Slavonic *бл҃годꙋ́шїе*). When Saint Theoctistus reposed in extreme old age (ca. 451/467), Saint Euthymius came to the monastery and presided over his burial, as recorded in Cyril of Scythopolis's *Life of Saint Euthymius*.

[^33]: *Establish our faithful king (вѣ́рнагѡ королѧ̀ на́шего оу҆твердѝ)*: In the 1761 Pochaiv Menaion, published under the jurisdiction of the Basilian Order within the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, this petition in the festal Theotokion explicitly names the Catholic monarch of Poland (Augustus III of Poland, Elector of Saxony, r. 1734–1763) rather than the Russian Tsar or Emperor found in Moscow Synodal printings (*благовѣ́рныхъ царе́й на́шихъ* or *вѣ́рнагѡ импера́тора на́шего*).

[^34]: *O divine and God-wise and sacred pair of fathers, most radiant (Бж҃твеннаѧ и҆ бг҃омꙋ́драѧ и҆ сщ҃еннаѧ дво́ице ѻ҆тє́цъ пресвѣ́тлаѧ)*: The closing troparion of Ode 9 invokes the monastic dyad of the Judean wilderness: Saint Euthymius the Great and his disciple and co-founder Saint Theoctistus, whose coenobium in the wadi served as the foundational nursery for wilderness ascetics.

[^35]: *Idiomelon. By Ephraim of Caria (Самогла́сенъ. Є҆́фрема карі́йскагѡ)*: Ephraim of Caria (Greek: *Ἐφραὶμ ὁ Καρίας*), an 8th/9th-century Byzantine hymnographer whose idiomelic stichera for prominent martyrs and hierarchs are preserved in the Studite and Constantinopolitan liturgical traditions.

[^36]: *With those who suffered with thee (съ пострада́вшими съ тобо́ю)*: The 1761 Pochaiv edition prints the typographical defect *съ токо́ю* for *съ тобо́ю*. The hymn addresses Saint Anthimus together with the multitude of Christians of Nicomedia who suffered martyrdom alongside him under Diocletian and Maximian.

[^37]: *The Holy Hieromartyr Babylas... and the Holy Prophet and God-Seer Moses (Ст҃а́гѡ сщ҃енномꙋ́ченика вавѵ́лы... И҆ ст҃а́гѡ проро́ка и҆ бг҃ови́дца мѡѷсе́а)*: Saint Babylas was Bishop of Antioch (d. 250/251) during the Decian persecution; according to Saint John Chrysostom's renowned panegyric (*De Sancto Babyla contra Iulianum et Gentiles*), Babylas barred the pagan Roman Emperor (traditionally Philip the Arab or Decius) from entering the Christian assembly until he did penance for a murder. He was martyred together with three young Christian brothers whom he had catechized: Urban, Prilidian, and Epolonius (along with their mother Christodoule). The same day commemorates the Holy Prophet Moses, who beheld the divine glory on Mount Sinai and mediated the Old Covenant.

[^38]: *With innocent children (съ неѕло́бивыми ѻ҆́троки)*: An explicit reference to the three young boys—Urban (twelve years old), Prilidian (nine), and Epolonius (seven)—who were interrogated, tortured, and beheaded with Saint Babylas.

[^39]: *To be made a god over Pharaoh (є҆́же ѡ҆божи́тисѧ над̾ фараѡ́номъ, ꙗ҆́кѡ бг҃ъ)*: An explicit hymnographic allusion to Exodus 7:1: "And the Lord said unto Moses, See, I have made thee a god to Pharaoh: and Aaron thy brother shall be thy prophet."

[^40]: *Thou veilest evil-believing blindness, revealing a revelation unto the faithful (спокрыва́еши ѕловѣ́рное ѡ҆слѣпле́нїе, ꙗ҆влѧ́ѧ вѣ́рнымъ ѡ҆крове́нїе)*: An explicit allusion to Exodus 34:29–35 and 2 Corinthians 3:13–16, where Moses put a veil over his shining face when speaking to the people of Israel who could not gaze upon the divine glory.

[^41]: *Having seen His back parts... and through the cleft (ви́дѣвши за́днаѧ... и҆ чрез̾ скважню̀)*: A direct liturgical citation of Exodus 33:22–23, where God places Moses in a cleft of the rock and permits him to see His 'back parts' (*ὀπίσω μου* / *за́дняя*), patristically interpreted by the Church Fathers (Gregory of Nyssa, John Chrysostom) as the foreshadowing of God's revelation through the human nature of Christ in the Incarnation.

[^42]: *Behold, I and the children whom God hath given me (се́ а҆́зъ и҆ дѣ́ти ꙗ҆̀же мнѣ̀ дадѐ бг҃ъ)*: Citing Isaiah 8:18 (quoted also in Hebrews 2:13), applied liturgically to Saint Babylas and the three spiritual sons catechized and martyred with him.

[^43]: *Even as Simeon foretold (ꙗ҆́коже сѷмеѡ́нъ проречѐ)*: An allusion to Luke 2:35 ('Yea, a sword shall pierce through thy own soul also'), fulfilling Simeon's prophecy at the Crucifixion.

[^44]: *By John the Monk (Їѡа́нна мона́ха)*: Saint John of Damascus (ca. 676–749), monk of Saint Sabbas the Sanctified Lavra, author of the Tone 6 canon honoring Saint Babylas.

[^45]: *Moses, first of the prophets... and in the Theotokia, by Clement (Мѡѷсе́й проро́кѡвъ пе́рвый... Въ бг҃оро́дичныхъ же Кли́ментово)*: The Greek Menaion preserves this exact acrostic: *Μωσεὺς προφητῶν πρῶτος αἰνείσθω λόγοις*, with the Theotokia signed by Clement the Hymnographer (9th century).

[^46]: *Hidden by the knowledge of God as in an ark... therefore the queen and divine Providence reared thee (сокрове́но зна́нїемъ бж҃їимъ, ꙗ҆́коже въ ковче́зѣ... тѣ́мъ тѧ̀ воспита́ше цр҃ца)*: Allusion to Exodus 2:2–10, where the infant Moses was placed in an ark of bulrushes by the river's brink, discovered by Pharaoh's daughter (called 'queen' or 'princess' in Byzantine hymnography), and nurtured under divine Providence.

[^47]: *The Holy Spirit through an angel clearly taught thee the knowledge of ancient things (дх҃ъ ст҃ы́й чрез̾ а҆́гг҃ла, дре́внихъ навꙋчѝ тѧ̀ зна́нїю ꙗ҆́снѡ)*: Reflecting the patristic and Jewish tradition (attested in the Book of Jubilees and Patristic commentaries on Genesis) that an angel of the Lord revealed the primeval account of the six days of Creation and the patriarchal generations unto Moses during the forty days upon Mount Sinai.

[^48]: *When the slowness of thy tongue and thy voice was made clear (Оу҆гна́нїю ѧ҆зы́ка и҆ гла́са твоегѡ̀, оу҆ѧсни́вшꙋсѧ)*: An allusion to Exodus 4:10, where Moses professed: 'O my Lord, I am not eloquent... but I am slow of speech, and of a slow tongue', which God removed, inspiring him to set down the words of the book of Genesis (*бытїѧ̀ та́инъ бж҃їихъ*).

[^49]: *For God appeared unto thee as a Son (ꙗ҆ви́сѧ бо сы́нъ тебѣ̀ бг҃ъ)*: In Patristic Christological typology (e.g. Justin Martyr, Irenaeus, John Chrysostom), all theophanic visions of the Old Testament—including the burning bush and Sinai—were manifestations of the pre-incarnate Son and Word of God.

[^50]: *If Thou hast known me above all... manifest Thyself intelligently unto me (а҆́ще позна́лъ є҆сѝ мѧ̀ па́че всѣ́хъ... разꙋ́мнѡ мнѣ̀ ꙗ҆ви́сѧ)*: A liturgical paraphrase of Exodus 33:12–13: 'Yet Thou hast said, I know thee by name, and thou hast also found grace in My sight. Now therefore, I pray Thee, if I have found grace in Thy sight, shew me now Thy way, that I may know Thee.'

[^51]: *Thou didst destroy the seven nations of Canaanite malice (се́дмь оу҆́бѡ ѕлѡ́бы ханане́йскїѧ ѧ҆зы́ки погꙋби́лъ є҆сѝ)*: Referring to Deuteronomy 7:1: 'seven nations greater and mightier than thou; the Hittites, and the Girgashites, and the Amorites, and the Canaanites, and the Perizzites, and the Hivites, and the Jebusites.'

[^52]: *Tablets of the Law written with His finger (Пи́санныѧ скрижа̑ли пе́рстомъ є҆гѡ̀ зако́нныѧ)*: Citing Exodus 31:18: 'tables of stone, written with the finger of God.'

[^53]: *As an ark, covered everywhere with the rays of the Spirit as with gold (ковче́гъ бцⷣе, зарѧ́ми дх҃а всюдꙋ̀, ꙗ҆́кѡ зла́томъ покрове́нъ)*: Typological interpretation of the Ark of the Covenant overlaid with pure gold within and without (Exodus 25:11), prefiguring the all-pure Virgin sanctified by the Holy Spirit.

[^54]: *Korah was shown descending alive into Hades (коре́й... ꙗ҆́висѧ низше́дъ во а҆́дъ жи́въ)*: An explicit citation of the rebellion of Korah in Numbers 16:31–33, where the earth opened her mouth and swallowed up Korah, Dathan, and Abiram alive into the pit (*во а҆́дъ*), demonstrating God's vindication of Moses and Aaron's legitimate authority.

[^55]: *Michael the leader of the heavenly hosts appeared as the divine guardian of thy body (ꙗ҆ви́сѧ михаи́лъ нача́лникъ нбⷭныхъ, и҆ тѣ́ла твоегѡ̀ бж҃е́ственный храни́тель)*: A direct liturgical invocation of Jude 1:9: 'Yet Michael the archangel, when contending with the devil he disputed about the body of Moses, durst not bring against him a railing accusation, but said, The Lord rebuke thee.'

[^56]: *Aaron's divine rod showed thee forth budded (А҆арѡ́новъ тѧ̀ бж҃е́ственный же́злъ прозѧбнꙋ́ти показа̀)*: An allusion to Numbers 17:8, where Aaron's dry rod miraculously budded, blossomed, and yielded ripe almonds, an ancient patristic type of the Virgin Mary conceiving and giving birth without human seed.

[^57]: Ex. 33:18–23; cf. Lk. 9:28–36. On Mount Tabor Moses beheld the Incarnate Word in His divine glory face to face, no longer hidden as in the cleft of the rock on Sinai.

[^58]: Matt. 17:1–8; Lk. 9:28–36. The Tishbite Elijah represents the living (having been taken up alive into heaven, 4 Kgs. [2 Kgs.] 2:11), while Moses represents those from the dead.

[^59]: Heb. 9:2–5. Typological figures of the Theotokos: the golden jar containing the manna, the stone tablets of the Law, and the table of showbread.

[^60]: A classic post-Iconoclastic Byzantine Theotokion condemning iconoclasts to Gehenna, preserved intact in the Ruthenian and Studite Menaion recension.

[^61]: Ps. 15:3 (LXX) [Ps. 16:3].

[^62]: Ps. 15:8 (LXX) [Ps. 16:8].

[^63]: Ps. 33:18 (LXX) [Ps. 34:17].

[^64]: Ps. 32:1 (LXX) [Ps. 33:1].

[^65]: Cf. Lk. 1:76 (The Canticle of Zacharias, the *Benedictus*).

[^66]: Cf. Matt. 23:35 and Lk. 11:51: Zacharias the son of Barachias, slain between the temple and the altar.

[^67]: Lk. 1:8–13. Zacharias served in the order of Abijah (descendant of Aaron), when the Archangel Gabriel appeared unto him on the right side of the altar of incense.

[^68]: Ex. 28:33–35. The golden bells (*tintinnabula*) upon the hem of the high priest's robe, which sounded as he entered the sanctuary before the Lord.

[^69]: Ex. 28:4 (LXX *ποδήρης*, Slavonic *поди́ръ*), the high-priestly robe reaching down to the feet.

[^70]: Cf. Isa. 11:1. The Protoevangelium of James relates that the Virgin Mary was received in the temple by the high priest Zacharias.

[^71]: Lk. 1:9–11.

[^72]: Lk. 1:78: the Dayspring (*oriens*) from on high; Mal. 4:2 [LXX 3:20]: the Sun of righteousness.

[^73]: Lk. 1:39–40: the Visitation of the Theotokos to the house of Zacharias.

[^74]: Lk. 1:78–79: "...whereby the Dayspring from on high hath visited us, to give light to them that sit in darkness and the shadow of death, to guide our feet into the way of peace."

[^75]: Lk. 1:48: "For behold, from henceforth all generations shall call me blessed."

[^76]: Ex. 28:36–38. The high-priestly mitre or turban (*kydaris*, Greek *κίδαρις* / *μίτρα*), upon which was fixed a plate of pure gold engraved with "Holiness unto the Lord" (*Святы́ня Го́споду*).

[^77]: Lk. 1:6: "And they were both righteous before God, walking in all the commandments and ordinances of the Lord blameless."

[^78]: Ex. 28:15, 29–30. The breastplate of judgment (*hoshen mishpat*, Greek *λογεῖον τῶν κρίσεων*) worn over the heart of the high priest.

[^79]: Lk. 1:41–45. Elizabeth, filled with the Holy Spirit upon hearing Mary's salutation, prophesied: "Blessed art thou among women, and blessed is the fruit of thy womb."

[^80]: Cf. Prov. 9:2; 1 Cor. 10:16.

[^81]: Lk. 1:64: "And his mouth was opened immediately, and his tongue loosed, and he spake, and praised God."

[^82]: Jn. 3:29: the friend of the Bridegroom (*philos tou nymphíou*).

[^83]: Matt. 11:11: "Among them that are born of women there hath not risen a greater than John the Baptist."

[^84]: Lk. 1:67–68: "And his father Zacharias was filled with the Holy Ghost, and prophesied, saying, Blessed be the Lord God of Israel..."

[^85]: Lk. 1:20–22; Matt. 23:35. In early Christian and patristic tradition (e.g. Origen, Gregory of Nyssa), Zacharias was killed by Herod's soldiers between the temple and the altar when he refused to disclose the hiding place of the infant John.

[^86]: Matt. 23:35.

[^87]: Ps. 63:11 (LXX) [Ps. 64:10].

[^88]: Ps. 63:2 (LXX) [Ps. 64:1].

[^89]: Ps. 96:11 (LXX) [Ps. 97:11].

[^90]: Ps. 111:6b (LXX) [Ps. 112:6b].

[^91]: The Miracle at Chonae (ancient Colossae in Phrygia): the pagan inhabitants attempted to drown the church of the Archangel Michael by diverting the rivers Lykokapros and Kouroumphos into a single torrent. St. Michael struck the rock with his staff, splitting it open (*Chonae* meaning "funnels" or "clefts"), swallowing the waters and preserving the sanctuary and the custodian St. Archippus.

[^92]: St. Eudoxius, *kometes* (count) in the Roman army under Diocletian, martyred in Melitene of Armenia with Zeno, Macarius, and 1,104 fellow soldiers.

[^93]: St. Archippus of Chonae, who served as sanctuary-keeper (*paramonarios*) at the miraculous shrine of the Archangel Michael for sixty years.

[^94]: Ps. 103:4 (LXX) [Ps. 104:4]; Heb. 1:7.

[^95]: The common troparion of the Bodiless Powers, Tone 4.

[^96]: Ps. 150:5: "Praise Him upon the loud cymbals; praise Him upon the high sounding cymbals."

[^97]: The edicts of Diocletian (c. 303–304 AD) mandating sacrifices to the Roman imperial idols throughout the empire.

[^98]: A Greek paronomasia on the name *Eudoxios* (from *eu*, well/good, and *doxa*, glory or right opinion): "true to thy name, O glory (*doxa*) of the pious".

[^99]: St. Eudoxius (martyred ca. 311 under Licinius in Melitene, Armenia) was a military officer who, along with fellow soldiers Zeno and Macarius and 1,104 others, suffered martyrdom for refusing to offer sacrifice to pagan idols. The hymn echoes Isaiah 53:7 ("He was led as a lamb to the slaughter").

[^100]: Allusion to Psalm 113:12–16 (LXX) / Psalm 115:4–8 ("The idols of the nations are silver and gold, the works of the hands of men. They have mouths, and shall not speak...").

[^101]: The martyr contrasts corruptible temporal life with the truly existing eternal life gained through martyrdom in Christ.

[^102]: A patristic Christological summary: God the Word, co-eternal with the Father, assumed human nature hypostatically from the pure Virgin to renew Adam, the forefather of mankind.

[^103]: The Slavonic text employs "свѣ́тная жи́знь" (worldly/secular life), contrasting the fleeting allurements of this world with eternal life.

[^104]: Allusion to Romans 8:18 ("For I reckon that the sufferings of this present time are not worthy to be compared with the glory which shall be revealed in us").

[^105]: Direct citation of Psalm 113:16 (LXX) / Psalm 134:18 (LXX) ("Let them that make them become like unto them, and all that trust in them").

[^106]: Christ addressed as "the treasure of petitions" (сокро́вище проше́нїй), the inexhaustible source of grace who answers the prayers of His martyrs.

[^107]: The martyr proclaims his unshakeable trust in God against the imperial inquisitor's threats of torture by sword and fire.

[^108]: Allusion to Romans 8:35 ("Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? Shall tribulation, or distress, or persecution, or famine, or nakedness, or peril, or sword?").

[^109]: The printed text contains a minor orthographic slip ("Ѻ҆́гнагѡ"), an apparent scribal assimilation from "ѻ҆гнѧ̀" for "ѻ҆́нагѡ" ("of that [unquenchable fire]").

[^110]: Allusion to Psalm 113:13–15 (LXX) / Psalm 115:5–7 ("They have mouths, and shall not speak: eyes have they, and shall not see: they have ears, and shall not hear... neither shall they walk").

[^111]: According to his Life, before his arrest St. Eudoxius distributed his earthly estate to the poor and needy, storing up treasure in heaven.

[^112]: Allusion to the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3), who refused to bow to Nebuchadnezzar's golden image.

[^113]: Divine grace as the unvanquished armor enabling the martyr to conquer demonic hordes.

[^114]: The martyr's contempt for temporal earthly rank in anticipation of the eternal heavenly reward.

[^115]: The classical patristic metaphor of navigating the stormy sea of tortures to anchor safely in the harbor of eternal life.

[^116]: Allusion to Psalm 65:12 (LXX) ("We went through fire and through water: and Thou broughtest us out into a place of refreshment").

[^117]: The "noetic serpent" (мы́сленный ѕмі́й) is the traditional Byzantine and Slavonic patristic designation for the devil (cf. Rev. 12:9).

[^118]: The hymn emphasizes the victory of Christ's martyr, who despite the fragility of mortal human flesh, overthrew the bodiless demonic powers through faith in the Holy Trinity.

[^119]: The martyr crowned with the robe of incorruption by the celestial angelic orders.

[^120]: The printed Slavonic text reads "страда́ній созда́нїе" (creation of sufferings), which is an apparent typographical slip for "возда́нїе" (recompense/reward of sufferings), as attested in parallel Greek and Slavonic Menaion witnesses.

[^121]: Patristic Theotokion alluding to Psalm 44:2 (LXX) ("My heart hath poured forth a good word"), traditionally applied to the eternal generation of the Son and Word from the Father.

[^122]: A hymnographic wordplay on the name of St. Eudoxius (from Greek Εὐδόξιος, "of good glory/fame"), who exchanged worldly renown for the eternal glory of martyrdom.

[^123]: St. Eudoxius was beheaded in Melitene under Emperor Licinius ca. 311.

[^124]: Allusion to Hebrews 11:26 ("Esteeming the reproach of Christ greater riches than the treasures in Egypt") and Hebrews 12:4 ("Ye have not yet resisted unto blood").

[^125]: The Canon of the Archangel Michael is an acrostic hymn composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (ca. 816–886): "Пе́рваго пою̀ безтѣле́сныхъ оу҆мѡ́въ: Їѡ́сифъ" ("I sing of the first of the bodiless minds: Joseph").

[^126]: St. Michael the Archistrategos invoked as the heavenly commander and spiritual foundation of all believers who seek his aid.

[^127]: God the Father addressed as "the pre-unoriginate Mind" (Оу҆́мъ пребезнача́лный), Who established Michael over the celestial hierarchy.

[^128]: The Archangel Michael portrayed as an invincible rampart and spiritual weapon defending the Church on earth against demonic forces.

[^129]: The archangel's swiftest angelic oversight traversing creation to deliver the faithful from perils.

[^130]: St. Michael as heavenly guide and instructor of the erring.

[^131]: Traditional Dionysian angelic theology: holy angels as untarnished spiritual mirrors reflecting the uncreated divine light of the Holy Spirit.

[^132]: The primary festal commemoration of September 6: the Miracle of the Archangel Michael at Chonae (Colossae) in Phrygia, where he protected his holy spring and church from being destroyed by pagan inundation.

[^133]: The creation of the fiery angelic orders out of nothing (ex nihilo) by the Word of God.

[^134]: Stavrotheotokion: the Mother of God weeping before the Cross, contemplating the voluntary passion and condescension of her Son.

[^135]: God the primal Mind creating the celestial intellects and illuminating St. Michael through direct contemplation and participation in the divine glory.

[^136]: The celestial hierarchy according to the Dionysian taxonomy (Thrones, Dominions, Powers, Authorities, Principalities).

[^137]: The Archangel Michael given by God as an insurmountable protective wall and shield for Christians.

[^138]: Prophecy of Habakkuk 3:3 ("God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from the mount of shade [overshadowed mountain]"), interpreted patristically as the Incarnation of Christ from the Virgin.

[^139]: Allusion to Exodus 23:20–23 ("Behold, I send an Angel before thee, to keep thee in the way, and to bring thee into the place which I have prepared").

[^140]: Angelic deification (theosis) through direct participation in the divine energies and radiances of God.

[^141]: The miraculous shrine of the Archangel Michael at Chonae, famed throughout Christendom as a wellspring of physical and spiritual healings.

[^142]: The union of heaven and earth achieved through the Incarnation of God the Word.

[^143]: The Archistrategos Michael intervening on behalf of Israel in the Exodus and Joshua's campaigns (Exod. 14:19; Josh. 5:13–15).

[^144]: Allusion to Joshua 5:13–15, where the "captain of the host of the Lord" appeared unto Joshua outside Jericho.

[^145]: The canonical Kontakion of the Archangel Michael in Tone 2.

[^146]: Allusion to Luke 15:7, 10 ("Joy shall be in heaven over one sinner that repents").

[^147]: Allusion to Daniel 10:13–21, where the Archangel Gabriel reveals the assistance of Michael ("one of the chief princes"), conflating the riverside visions of Daniel (Dan. 10:4 at the Tigris) and Ezekiel (Ezek. 1:1 by the river Chebar/Chobar).

[^148]: The traditional Byzantine synaxis honoring both Archangels Michael and Gabriel as foremost guardians of mankind.

[^149]: St. Michael as leader of the immaterial celestial hosts.

[^150]: The cosmic song of praise drawn from Daniel 3 / The Song of the Three Holy Children.

[^151]: Michael and Gabriel depicted metaphorically as twin spiritual luminaries reflecting the light of the Holy Trinity.

[^152]: The healing power of the sanctuary at Chonae dispelling illness by the miraculous presence of the Archistrategos.

[^153]: The complementary ministrations of the Archangels Michael (warfare/protection) and Gabriel (proclamation/joy) on behalf of humanity.

[^154]: Eucharistic and liturgical communion uniting angels and men in common festal celebration.

[^155]: The archangels invoked as intercessors before the throne of the Trinity for remission of sins.

[^156]: The Miracle of Chonae: pagans attempted to divert the Lykokapros and Kouron rivers to destroy the church of St. Michael, but the Archangel struck the rock with his staff, splitting it open to swallow the floodwaters.

[^157]: Stichera at the Praises echoing the Thrice-Holy hymn chanted perpetually by the seraphim and archangels (Isa. 6:3; Rev. 4:8).

[^158]: The parrhesia (boldness) of the Archistrategos Michael standing before the throne of God to plead for those in distress.

[^159]: The annual liturgical commemoration of the Archangel Michael celebrated on September 6.

[^160]: Solemn festal Doxasticon calling all the faithful to offer the flowers of virtue to honor the assembly of the archangels.

[^161]: Stavrotheotokion citing Luke 2:35 ("Yea, a sword shall pierce through thy own soul also"), Simeon's prophecy at the Presentation in the Temple.

[^162]: Allusion to Isaiah 14:12 (the fall of Lucifer / morning star) and Ephesians 6:16 ("the fiery darts of the wicked"), which are quenched through the archangel's intercession.

[^163]: Classical Byzantine Stavrotheotokion depicting the Mother of God following Christ to the Cross as a mournful ewe-lamb witnessing the slaughter of her Lamb.

[^164]: Prokeimenon citing Psalm 103:4 (LXX) / Hebrews 1:7.

[^165]: Liturgical appointments for the feast of the Miracle of the Archangel Michael at Colossae: Epistle to the Hebrews 2:2–10 (pericope 305); Gospel according to Luke 10:16–21 (pericope 51); and Communion Hymn from Psalm 103:4.

[^166]: Commemoration of the Forefeast of the Nativity of the Most Holy Theotokos, and the Holy Martyr Sozon (a shepherd of Lycaonia martyred under Maximian in Pompeiopolis, Cilicia, ca. 304).

[^167]: The metaphorical dawning of Christ, the Sun of Righteousness (Mal. 4:2), announced beforehand by the birth of the Virgin Mary.

[^168]: Forefestal hymnody anticipating the birth of the Virgin as the reversal of universal sorrow and the source of eschatological gladness.

[^169]: Typological fulfillment of the protoevangelion: through the birth of Mary from the righteous ancestors Joachim and Anna, the curse of Adam is loosed.

[^170]: St. Sozon's martyrdom viewed as triumph over Satan through the power of Christ (allusion to Gen. 3:15 and Rom. 16:20).

[^171]: St. Sozon arming himself with the invincible sign of the Cross and being translated to the celestial kingdom.

[^172]: Martyrdom narrative of St. Sozon enduring the fiery furnace and severe torments, subsequently becoming a source of physical and spiritual healings.

[^173]: Theotokion Idiomelon invoking the Mother of God as mediatress and constant advocate for Christians before her Son and God.

[^174]: Metaphor of the rod of Jesse (Isa. 11:1) blossoming from the aged and barren womb of St. Anna, causing the prophets and patriarchs to rejoice.

[^175]: Psalm 44:11 (LXX 44:10).

[^176]: Messianic ancestry celebration: David, Jesse, and the Levitical priesthood rejoicing at the loosening of Anna's barrenness.

[^177]: Psalm 44:13 (LXX 44:12).

[^178]: Chairetismoi (salutations) accumulating Old Testament typological titles of the Theotokos: temple, holy mountain, table, lampstand, tabernacle, and unburnt bush.

[^179]: Forefestal sticheron composed by St. Germanus, Patriarch of Constantinople (ca. 634–733).

[^180]: Common Troparion of a martyr applied to St. Sozon.

[^181]: Forefestal hymn highlighting Isaiah 11:1 and the miraculous birth of the Mother of God from the barren Anna.

[^182]: Triple doxological acclamation ("Glory to thy coming... Glory to thy nativity... Glory to thy virginity") addressed to the Theotokos.

[^183]: Catalogue of Marian Old Testament types: the burning bush of Moses (Ex. 3:2), the golden jar of manna (Heb. 9:4), Aaron's rod that budded (Num. 17:8), the overshadowing cloud (Ex. 40:34), the closed gate of Ezekiel (Ezek. 44:2), the royal throne (1 Kgs. 10:18), and the great mountain of Daniel (Dan. 2:34–35).

[^184]: Acrostic hymn by St. Joseph the Hymnographer celebrating the overthrow of the sorrow of Eve ("pramater" / the first mother).

[^185]: The birth of the Mother of God as the boundary and cessation of the sterile reign of sin.

[^186]: Christ as the Super-essential Word ("Presushchestvennyi" / Hyperousios) sanctifying humanity through the incarnation.

[^187]: The troparion concluded from Ode 1 of the Forefeast canon: Christ lifting up fallen humanity from the shadow of death into divine illumination.

[^188]: St. Sozon invoked to deliver the faithful from perils and sorrows through his intercession before the Benefactor and Lord.

[^189]: St. Sozon characterized as an all-radiant beacon illuminating the universal Church with the splendors of his martyrdom.

[^190]: Allusion to the Song of the Sea (Ex. 15:1–21) and Habakkuk 3:15: the blood of St. Sozon submerging the demonic host as Pharaoh's army was drowned in the Red Sea.

[^191]: The Mother of God portrayed as fruitful earth bearing the wheat of eternal life and the Cultivator of all good things.

[^192]: Messianic typology of the rod and blossom (Isa. 11:1), where Christ is the Husbandman pruning away evil shoots.

[^193]: Typology of the unhewn mountain and the noetic corner stone (Dan. 2:34–35, 45) shattering pagan idols.

[^194]: Prophetic shadows of the Old Law fulfilled in the virginal conception of the divine Lawgiver.

[^195]: The shed blood of St. Sozon quenching the fire of pagan idolatry.

[^196]: St. Sozon's steadfast endurance of torture compared to an unshakeable column overturning pagan deception.

[^197]: Davidic typological parallel (1 Kgs. 17 / 1 Sam. 17): St. Sozon slaying the spiritual Goliath and demonic hordes with the sling of faith.

[^198]: Kontakion of St. Sozon celebrating him as a seasoned champion of piety and dispenser of healings.

[^199]: Ikos recounting St. Sozon overturning the savagery of pagan idolaters in Cilicia and confirming believers in salvation.

[^200]: Sessional hymn invoking St. Sozon as a tranquil harbor quenching spiritual and bodily fevers.

[^201]: Festal kathisma echoing Psalm 95:11 (LXX) and Isaiah 11:1 on the cosmic joy of heaven and earth at the dawning of the Theotokos.

[^202]: The Nativity of the Virgin as the dawn of purification, sanctification, and perfect redemption for the human race.

[^203]: The barren womb of Anna blessed to bring forth the mother of the uncontainable Logos.

[^204]: The dispelling of demonic darkness by the ensouled cloud of Christ the Sun of Righteousness.

[^205]: Allusion to Psalm 84:11 (LXX 84:10): "Mercy and truth have met together," fulfilled in the Virgin as a fruitful olive tree (Ps. 51:10 / LXX 51:8).

[^206]: St. Sozon consuming pagan idolatry like dry brushwood through the divine fervor of his words.

[^207]: Hagiographical episode of St. Sozon entering the pagan temple of Artemis in Pompeiopolis and breaking the golden hand of the idol to distribute to the poor.

[^208]: The martyrium of St. Sozon recognized as a sanctuary of miraculous healing for the afflicted.

[^209]: Typology of the sealed book (Isa. 29:11; Rev. 5:1) unreadable by human nature, wherein the divine Logos is inscribed.

[^210]: Conclusion of the troparion from Ode 5: Christ as the divine grape cluster yielding the spiritual wine of salvation.

[^211]: The birth of the Theotokos as the cosmic replacement of sorrow with eschatological gladness.

[^212]: Prophetic typology of the scroll (Ezek. 2:9; Isa. 8:1) upon which the Father's finger inscribes the names of the redeemed.

[^213]: St. Sozon illuminated by the light of the Holy Trinity dispelling pagan polytheism.

[^214]: Martyrdom imagery of St. Sozon: the shedding of his blood depicted as a blooming rose filling the world with spiritual fragrance.

[^215]: St. Sozon as a noetic grape of the True Vine (John 15:1) producing the wine of compunction.

[^216]: Old Testament typological figures of the Virgin: Jacob's ladder (Gen. 28:12), the bridge to the celestial realm, and the mountain of God (Ps. 67:16 / LXX 67:15).

[^217]: Metaphor of the imperial purple: St. Anna as the scarlet dye and the Virgin as the royal purple fabric woven for the incarnation of Christ the King.

[^218]: The Virgin as a fountain emerging from a drop to swallow up the deluge of pagan polytheism.

[^219]: The miraculous sprouting from Anna's barren root uprooting the ancestral thorns of malice (allusion to Gen. 3:18).

[^220]: St. Sozon presenting himself as a living sacrifice upon the altar of God (allusion to Rom. 12:1).

[^221]: St. Sozon safely navigating the tempestuous sea of worldly delusion into the celestial haven.

[^222]: Martyrdom account: St. Sozon tortured with iron scrapers before commending his soul into the hands of God.

[^223]: Kontakion of the Forefeast celebrating the birth of Mary as the heavenly bridal chamber and vehicle of the Logos.

[^224]: Ikos summoning believers to contemplate the infant Mary in the arms of Anna and Joachim as the restoration of creation.

[^225]: Beatitude of Joachim and Anna whose daughter brought forth the uncreated Word.

[^226]: Joachim and Anna enriched through the gift of the Theotokos, who bore the Giver of all divine gifts.

[^227]: Song of Songs 1:2 allusion applied to St. Anna nursing the Mother of the divine Provider.

[^228]: King David summoned to rejoice at the dawning of the new Ark of the Covenant (Ps. 131:8 / LXX 131:8).

[^229]: Daniel 3 typology: St. Sozon in the furnace of torture receiving refreshing dew from God.

[^230]: Historical martyrdom event: St. Sozon, possessing a soul purer than gold, refusing to worship the golden idol of Artemis in Cilicia.

[^231]: St. Sozon's earthly occupation as a shepherd elevated: Christ the Chief Shepherd incorporates him into His spiritual sheepfold beyond the reach of demonic wolves.

[^232]: Doxological hymnody from Ode 8 celebrating the incarnation through the Holy Spirit.

[^233]: Prophetic fulfillment: the prophets rejoicing as the nativity of Mary brings their messianic announcements to realization.

[^234]: Cosmic and liturgical chorus: mountains, apostles, martyrs, monastics, and righteous rejoicing at the Mother of God's birth.

[^235]: Conclusion of the troparion from Ode 8 celebrating Mary as the perfumed blossom dispelling the stench of the ancestral transgression.

[^236]: The barren condition of fallen humanity transformed by the birth of the Theotokos.

[^237]: St. Sozon breaking the golden hand of the idol of Artemis in Cilicia and distributing the pieces to the poor, thereby storing up eternal spiritual riches.

[^238]: St. Sozon's spiritual combat: wrestling in human flesh against the bodiless demonic powers through the grace of Christ.

[^239]: Psalm 80:11 (LXX 80:10) allusion: "Open thy mouth wide, and I will fill it," fulfilled in St. Sozon confounding pagan blasphemers.

[^240]: Patristic and psalmographic typologies: Mary as the holy city of God (Ps. 86:3 / LXX 86:3) and the portal of restoration to paradise.

[^241]: The Virgin as the golden candlestick (Ex. 25:31) bearing Christ, the Dayspring from on high (Luke 1:78), dispelling demonic night.

[^1111]: Physical leaf p166 (Fol. 82v). Doxastikon (Glory, now and ever) at the Aposticha of Small Vespers, Tone 6.

[^1112]: Physical leaf p166 (Fol. 82v). Troparion (Troparion) of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross, Tone 1. In the Pochaiv 1761 edition, the text explicitly commemorates "our right-believing King" (бл҃говѣ́рному коро́лю на́шему, Augustus III of Poland).

[^1113]: Physical leaf p166 (Fol. 82v). Rubric detailing the solemn transfer of the Precious Cross from the table of oblation (prothesis) to the holy table (altar) after Small Vespers.

[^1114]: Physical leaf p167 (Fol. 83r). Rubric on the solemn deposition of the Precious Cross upon the holy table and placing the Gospel book in the high place.

[^1115]: Physical leaf p167 (Fol. 83r). Typikon rubric governing Great Vespers when the Feast of the Exaltation falls on a Sunday.

[^1116]: Physical leaf p167 (Fol. 83r). First sticheron at "Lord, I have cried", Tone 6, Podoben: "Having set aside all" (Всѐ ѿло́жше).

[^1117]: Physical leaf p167 (Fol. 83r). Second sticheron at "Lord, I have cried", Tone 6.

[^1118]: Physical leaf p167 (Fol. 83r). Third sticheron at "Lord, I have cried", Tone 6.

[^1119]: Physical leaf p168 (Fol. 83v). Doxastikon (Glory, now and ever) at "Lord, I have cried", Tone 2.

[^1120]: Physical leaf p168 (Fol. 83v). Great Prokeimenon (Psalm 113:11 LXX) prescribed when the Feast falls on a Saturday.

[^1121]: Physical leaf p168 (Fol. 83v). First Paremia for the Feast: Exodus 15:22–16:1 (LXX), the sweetening of the bitter waters of Marah by the wood.

[^1122]: Physical leaf p169 (Fol. 84r). Second Paremia for the Feast of the Holy Cross: Proverbs 3:11–18, concluding on p169 with the praise of Wisdom as the Tree of Life.

[^1123]: Physical leaf p169 (Fol. 84r). Third Paremia for the Feast of the Holy Cross: Isaiah 60:11–16, prophesying the glory of Jerusalem and the wood of Lebanon (cypress, pine, and cedar) to adorn the sanctuary.

[^1124]: Physical leaf p169 (Fol. 84r). First sticheron at the Litiya of the Exaltation, Tone 1, by St. Andrew of Jerusalem.

[^1125]: Physical leaf p170 (Fol. 84v). Second sticheron at the Litiya, concluding on p170.

[^1126]: Physical leaf p170 (Fol. 84v). Third sticheron at the Litiya, commemorating Jacob's crosswise blessing of Ephraim and Manasseh (Genesis 48:13–14) and petitioning for victories for the Christ-loving King.

[^1127]: Physical leaf p170 (Fol. 84v). Fourth sticheron at the Litiya, Tone 2, by St. Theophanes the Graptos, commemorating the vision of the Cross granted to Emperor Constantine and its discovery by St. Helena.

[^1128]: Physical leaf p170 (Fol. 84v). Fifth sticheron at the Litiya, Tone 2, by Cyprian.

[^1129]: Physical leaf p170 (Fol. 84v). Sixth sticheron at the Litiya, Tone 2, by Leo the Despot (Emperor Leo VI the Wise).

[^1130]: Physical leaf p170 (Fol. 84v). Seventh sticheron at the Litiya, Tone 4.

[^1131]: Church Slavonic: *Свѣтосїѧ́ненъ ѕвѣзда́ми ѡ҆́бразъ предпоказа̀ крⷭ҇те...* Sticheron at the Litiya attributed to Anatolios, celebrating the vision of the Cross granted to Saint Constantine the Great before the Battle of the Milvian Bridge and its finding by his mother Saint Helen.

[^1132]: Church Slavonic: *Чⷭ҇тна́гѡ крⷭ҇та, хрⷭ҇тѐ, дѣ́йство проwбрази́въ мѡѷсе́й...* Doxasticon at the Litiya referencing Exodus 17:11–12, where Moses' raised arms formed the figure of the Cross to overcome Amalek.

[^1133]: Church Slavonic: *Ра́дуйсѧ живоно́сный крⷭ҇те...* Automelon sticheron at the Aposticha, Tone 5.

[^1134]: Psalm 98:5 (LXX).

[^1135]: Church Slavonic: *Ра́дуйсѧ гдⷭ҇нь крⷭ҇те, и҆́мже разрѣши́ся ѿ клѧ́твы чл҃вѣ́чество...* Second sticheron at the Aposticha prosomoion to *Ра́дуйсѧ живоно́сный крⷭ҇те*.

[^1136]: Psalm 73:12 (LXX).

[^1137]: Church Slavonic: *Ра́дуйсѧ слѣпы́хъ наста́вниче...* Third sticheron at the Aposticha, completing the triadic set of prosomoia on Tone 5.

[^1138]: Church Slavonic: *Є҆го́же дре́вле мѡѷсе́й проwбразова́въ собо́ю...* Doxasticon at the Aposticha attributed to Saint John the Monk (Saint John Damascene).

[^1139]: Church Slavonic: *коро́лю на́шемꙋ, [и҆́мⷬк]*. In the 1761 Pochaiv edition, the text prints a rubrical blank/formula space (*имярек* / placeholder) for the reigning monarch of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth (King Augustus III of Poland, r. 1734–1763).

[^1140]: The prescribed Patristic reading between Vespers and Matins on the Exaltation of the Cross, typically from Saint John Chrysostom or Saint Theodore the Studite.

[^1141]: Church Slavonic: *То́кмѡ водрꙋзи́сѧ дре́во хрⷭ҇тѐ крⷭ҇та твоегѡ̀...* First Kathisma Sessional Hymn, Tone 6.

[^1142]: Church Slavonic: *Крⷭ҇та̀ твоегѡ̀ дре́вꙋ покланѧ́емсѧ...* Sessional Hymn on Tone 4 modeled on the automelon *Ка́мени запеча́танꙋ*.

[^1143]: Church Slavonic: *Дне́сь проро́ческое и҆спо́лнисѧ сло́во...* Second Kathisma Sessional Hymn, Tone 6, citing Psalm 131:7 (LXX): "We will worship at the place where His feet stood."

[^1144]: Church Slavonic: *Проwбража́ше та́инственнѡ дре́вле і҆исꙋ́съ навѵ́нъ...* Sessional Hymn on Tone 8 modeled on *Повелѣ́нное та́йнѡ*, referencing Joshua 10:12–14.

[^1145]: Church Slavonic: *Въ раи́ мѧ пре́жде дре́во ѡ҆бнажѝ...* Polyeleos Sessional Hymn on Tone 8, modeled on *Повелѣ́нное та́йнѡ*.

[^1146]: Psalm 97:3b (LXX).

[^1147]: Psalm 97:1a (LXX).

[^1148]: Church Slavonic: *Крⷭ҇те хрⷭ҇то́въ, хрⷭ҇тїа́нъ оу҆пова́нїе...* Post-Gospel Sticheron on Tone 6.

[^1149]: Church Slavonic: *Крⷭ҇тъ начерта́въ мѡѷсе́й...* Heirmos of Ode 1 of the Canon of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist (of Maiuma).

[^1150]: Church Slavonic: *Ѡ҆́бразъ дре́вле мѡѷсе́й...* First troparion of Ode 1 of the Canon of the Cross.

[^1151]: Church Slavonic: *Возложѝ мѡѷсе́й, на столпѣ̀ враче́вство...* Second troparion of Ode 1, referencing Numbers 21:8–9 and the bronze serpent set upon a pole.

[^1152]: Church Slavonic: *Показа̀ нб҃о крⷭ҇та̀ побѣ́дꙋ...* Third troparion of Ode 1, commemorating Saint Constantine's celestial vision of the sign of the Cross.

[^1153]: Church Slavonic: *Же́злъ во ѡ҆́бразъ та́йны прїе́млетсѧ...* Heirmos of Ode 3, drawing upon the typology of Aaron's rod that budded (Numbers 17:8).

[^1154]: Church Slavonic: *Ꙗ҆́кѡ и҆спꙋстѝ оу҆дарѧ́емь во́дꙋ...* First troparion of Ode 3, referencing the rock struck by Moses at Horeb (Exodus 17:6).

[^1155]: Church Slavonic: *Ре́брѡмъ пречⷭ҇тымъ, копіе́мъ прободе́ннымъ...* Second troparion of Ode 3, referencing John 19:34.

[^1156]: Church Slavonic: *Въ тебѣ̀ требл҃же́нне и҆ жизнода́вче крⷭ҇те...* Sessional Hymn after Ode 3, Tone 4, modeled on *Ско́рѡ предварѝ*.

[^1157]: Church Slavonic: *Оу҆слы́шахъ гдⷭ҇и смотре́нїа твоегѡ̀ та́инство...* Heirmos of Ode 4, drawing from the biblical canticle of Habakkuk 3:2.

[^1158]: Church Slavonic: *Горькоро́дныѧ преложѝ дре́вомъ мѡѷсе́й...* First troparion of Ode 4, referencing Marah (Exodus 15:23–25).

[^1159]: Church Slavonic: *Глꙋбинѣ̀ внѣдри́вый сѣкꙋ́щꙋю...* Second troparion of Ode 4, referencing Elisha causing the iron axe head to float by casting wood into the Jordan (4 Kings [2 Kings] 6:5–7).

[^1160]: Church Slavonic: *Сщ҃е́ннѡ ѡ҆полча́ютсѧ...* Third troparion of Ode 4, referencing the four-fold cruciform encampment of the twelve tribes of Israel around the Tabernacle (Numbers 2).

[^1161]: Church Slavonic: *Чꙋ́днѡ простира́емь, со́лнечныѧ лꙋчы̀ и҆спꙋща́ше крⷭ҇тъ...* Fourth troparion of Ode 4, referencing Psalm 18:2 (LXX) and the luminous apparition of the Cross in the heavens.

[^1162]: Church Slavonic: *Ѡ҆ требл҃же́нное дре́во...* Heirmos of Ode 5.

[^1163]: Church Slavonic: *Тебѐ приснопѣ́тое дре́во...* First troparion of Ode 5, referencing Genesis 3:24 and the flaming sword at Eden.

[^1164]: Church Slavonic: *Подзе́мныхъ си́лы проти̑вныѧ крⷭ҇та̀ страша́тсѧ...* Second troparion of Ode 5, echoing Philippians 2:10.

[^1165]: Church Slavonic: *Зарѧ́ми нетлѣ́нными ꙗ҆́вльсѧ бж҃твенный крⷭ҇тъ...* Third troparion of Ode 5.

[^1166]: Church Slavonic: *Во́днагѡ ѕвѣ́рѧ во оу҆тро́бѣ...* Heirmos of Ode 6, typologically interpreting Jonah's three days in the whale (Jonah 2) as a cruciform prefiguration.

[^1167]: Church Slavonic: *Ста́ростїю преклони́всѧ...* First troparion of Ode 6, referencing Jacob crossing his hands to bless Ephraim and Manasseh (Genesis 48:13–19).

[^1168]: Church Slavonic: *На ю҆́ныѧ возложи́въ дла́ни бж҃твенный і҆и҃ль...* Second troparion of Ode 6, interpreting Jacob's preference for the younger Ephraim as the Church superseding the Synagogue.

[^1169]: Church Slavonic: *бл҃говѣ́рнаго королѧ́ на́шего* [with marginal/interlinear liturgical variant: *вѣ́рныѧ лю́ди твоѧ̑*]. In the 1761 Pochaiv text, the original Royal printed formula references the reigning monarch of Poland-Lithuania (*королѧ́*), with the alternative congregational variant (*вѣ́рныѧ лю́ди*) noted.

[^1170]: Church Slavonic: *И҆́же до тре́тїѧгѡ нб҃сѐ восхище́нъ бы́сть...* Ikos of the Feast, citing 2 Corinthians 12:2–4 and Galatians 6:14.

[^1171]: Church Slavonic: *Безꙋ́мное велѣ́нїе мꙋчи́телѧ...* Heirmos of Ode 7, referencing the Three Holy Youths in the fiery furnace (Daniel 3).

[^1172]: Church Slavonic: *Дре́ва вкꙋси́въ пе́рвый въ человѣ́цѣхъ...* First troparion of Ode 7.

[^1173]: Church Slavonic: *Разрꙋшѝ повелѣ́нїе бж҃їе преслꙋша́нїе...* Second troparion of Ode 7, contrasting Adam's fall with the penitent thief entering Paradise (Luke 23:42–43).

[^1174]: Church Slavonic: *Же́злъ ѡ҆б̾е́млетъ кра́й і҆ѡ́сифова...* Third troparion of Ode 7, referencing Genesis 47:31 and Hebrews 11:21 (Jacob worshipping leaning on the top of his staff).

[^1175]: Church Slavonic: *Бл҃гослови́те ѻ҆́троцы...* Heirmos of Ode 8.

[^1176]: Church Slavonic: *тесѧ хрⷭ҇то́вꙋ крⷭ҇тꙋ̀...* Conclusion of the first troparion of Ode 8.

[^1177]: Church Slavonic: *Земноро́днїи дла́нми, строи́телїе бл҃года́ти крⷭ҇тъ...* Second troparion of Ode 8.

[^1178]: Church Slavonic: *Бж҃твеннымъ сꙋдо́мъ пред̾избра́ннїи весели́теся...* Third troparion of Ode 8.

[^1179]: Church Slavonic: *Велича́й дꙋшѐ моѧ̀, пречестны́й крⷭ҇тъ гдⷭ҇нь.* Festal Megalynarion (refrain) sung at Ode 9 in place of the *Magnificat* (*Честнѣ́йшꙋю*).

[^1180]: Church Slavonic: *Та́инъ є҆сѝ бцⷣе ра́й...* Heirmos of Ode 9 of the first Canon of the Cross.

[^1181]: Church Slavonic: *Да возра́дꙋютсѧ древа̀ дꙋбра́внаѧ всѧ̑...* First troparion of Ode 9, drawing from Psalm 95:12 (LXX): "Then shall all the trees of the forest rejoice."

[^1182]: Church Slavonic: *Сщ҃е́нный воста̀ ро́гъ, и҆ глава̀ всѣ̑мъ бг҃омꙋ́дрымъ крⷭ҇тъ...* Second troparion of Ode 9, drawing upon the typology of the horn of salvation and Psalm 74:11 (LXX).

[^1183]: Church Slavonic: *Велича́й дꙋшѐ моѧ̀, животворѧ́щагѡ крⷭ҇та̀ гдⷭ҇нѧ воздвиже́нїе.* Second festal refrain at Ode 9, accompanying the second canon.

[^1184]: Church Slavonic: *Снѣ́дїю дре́ва, ро́дꙋ прибы́вшаѧ сме́рть...* Heirmos of Ode 9 of the second Canon of the Cross.

[^1185]: Church Slavonic: *Го́рести оу҆бі́йственныѧ, ꙗ҆́же ѿ дре́ва...* First troparion of the second canon at Ode 9.

[^1186]: Church Slavonic: *Непреста́ннѡ грꙋжа́емыѧ мра́комъ пра́ѻтца гдⷭ҇и...* Second troparion of the second canon at Ode 9.

[^1187]: Church Slavonic: *Да ѡ҆́бразъ пока́жеши мі́рꙋ покланѧ́емый...* Third troparion of the second canon at Ode 9.

[^1188]: Church Slavonic: *Крⷭ҇тъ, храни́тель всеѧ̀ вселе́нныѧ...* The classic Festal Exapostilarion of the Cross, modeled on *Оу҆чени́кѡмъ*. The phrase *крⷭ҇тъ, царе́й держа́ва* ("the Cross is the dominion of kings") appears parenthesized in the 1761 Pochaiv print.

[^1189]: Church Slavonic: *Крⷭ҇тъ воздви́жетсѧ дне́сь, и҆ мі́ръ ѡ҆свѧща́етсѧ...* Theotokion / Doxasticon Exapostilarion, modeled on *Же́ны оу҆слы́шите*.

[^1190]: Church Slavonic: *Ѡ҆ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ...* Automelon sticheron at the Praises, Tone 8.

[^1191]: Church Slavonic: *Ѡ҆ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ, ꙗ҆́кѡ гро́здъ и҆спо́лненъ живота̀...* Second sticheron at the Praises.

[^1192]: Church Slavonic: *Ѡ҆ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ, широта̀ крⷭ҇та̀ и҆ долгота̀...* Third sticheron at the Praises, echoing Ephesians 3:18.

[^1193]: Church Slavonic: *Дне́сь происхо́дитъ крⷭ҇тъ гдⷭ҇нь...* Doxasticon at the Praises, Tone 6.

[^1194]: Church Slavonic: *съ вѣ́твьми васі́лїка ѕѣ́лїѧ, на подно́жїи креста̀ ѡ҆бложе́нными*. The rubrics of the 1761 Pochaiv Menaion explicitly prescribe that the Holy Cross is laid on the Tetrapod adorned with sweet basil herbs (*vasilika*), reflecting the ancient Jerusalem and Byzantine custom retained in the Ruthenian Church.

[^1195]: Church Slavonic: *ѡ҆ ст҃ѣ́йшемъ вселе́нскомъ а҆рхїере́и и҆́мⷬкъ : па́пѣ ры́мскомъ, бл҃гочести́вомъ и҆ бг҃охрани́момъ коро́ли на́шемъ, и҆́мⷬкъ*. The Pontifical petition directed toward the South, invoking Pope Clement XIII (r. 1758–1769) and King Augustus III of Poland (r. 1734–1763) as codified following the Synod of Zamość (1720).

[^1196]: Church Slavonic: *протоа҆рхїмандри́тѣ и҆́мⷬкъ : и҆ настоѧ́тели и҆́мⷬкъ : и҆лѝ и҆гꙋ́менѣ и҆́мⷬкъ*. Specific Basilian monastic commemorations for the Proto-archimandrite of the Order of Saint Basil the Great (OSBM) and local superiors.

[^1197]: Church Slavonic: *Прїиди́те вѣ́рнїи, животво́рѧщемꙋ дре́вꙋ поклони́мсѧ...* The first Idiomelon Sticheron at the Veneration of the Precious Cross, Tone 2.

[^1198]: p. 181 (Fol. 90r). Idiomelon sticheron of the Precious Cross sung during the veneration, concluding the first sticheron begun on fol. 89v.

[^1199]: p. 181 (Fol. 90r). Idiomelon sticheron in Tone 5 sung at the veneration of the Precious Cross.

[^1200]: p. 181 (Fol. 90r). Idiomelon sticheron in Tone 5, weaving together the prophecies of Isaiah (Isa. 66:18-20) and David (Ps. 85:9).

[^1201]: p. 181 (Fol. 90r). The Church Slavonic text here reads explicitly 'королѧ̀ на́шегѡ' ('our king'), reflecting the political reality of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth under King Augustus III (reigned 1734–1763) in this 1761 Pochaiv printing, contrasting with Synodal Russian editions which read 'царе́й на́шихъ' ('our tsars') or Greek editions reading 'τῶν βασιλέων ἡμῶν'.

[^1202]: p. 181 (Fol. 90r). Doxological conclusion of the Tone 6 Idiomelon sticheron, referencing Ps. 85:10.

[^1203]: p. 181 (Fol. 90r). Idiomelon sticheron in Tone 6 celebrating the cosmic redemption prefigured by the prophetic voices.

[^1204]: p. 181 (Fol. 90r). Prophetic citation of Deuteronomy 28:66 according to the Septuagint reading, traditionally interpreted patristically as a prophecy of Christ on the Cross.

[^1205]: p. 181 (Fol. 90r). Idiomelon sticheron in Tone 8 citing Psalm 73:12: 'Thou hast wrought salvation in the midst of the earth'.

[^1206]: p. 182 (Fol. 90v). Idiomelon sticheron in Tone 8 sung during the veneration of the Precious Cross, meditating on the cosmic paradoxes of the Crucifixion.

[^1207]: p. 182 (Fol. 90v). Doxastikon (Glory, Both now) at the veneration of the Cross in Tone 8: the Threnos (lamentation) of the Holy Virgin Mother of God at the foot of the Cross, echoing Simeon's prophecy (Luke 2:35) and Isaiah 53:2.

[^1208]: p. 182 (Fol. 90v). Liturgy of the Feast of the Universal Exaltation: First Festal Antiphon from Psalm 21 (LXX).

[^1209]: p. 182 (Fol. 90v). Liturgy of the Feast of the Universal Exaltation: Second Festal Antiphon from Psalm 73 (LXX).

[^1210]: p. 183 (Fol. 91r). Entrance Hymn (Isodikon) for the Feast of the Exaltation from Psalm 98:5 (LXX).

[^1211]: p. 183 (Fol. 91r). Hymn sung instead of the Trisagion at the Divine Liturgy on the Feast of the Exaltation and during the subsequent veneration.

[^1212]: p. 183 (Fol. 91r). Epistle reading for the Feast of the Exaltation: 1 Corinthians 1:18-24 (Slavonic Pericope 125), 'For the word of the cross is folly unto them that perish'.

[^1213]: p. 183 (Fol. 91r). Gospel reading for the Feast of the Exaltation: John 19:6-11, 13-20, 25-28, 30-35 (Slavonic Pericope 60), the Passion narrative according to John.

[^1214]: p. 183 (Fol. 91r). Communion Verse (Communion Hymn) for the Feast of the Exaltation from Psalm 4:7 (LXX).

[^1215]: p. 183 (Fol. 91r). Fasting rule (Typikon rubric) for the Feast of the Universal Exaltation of the Precious Cross: although a great Great Feast, it is kept as a strict fast day (wine and oil are permitted for the consolation of the brethren, but meat, dairy, eggs, and fish are strictly forbidden).

[^1216]: p. 184 (Fol. 91v). Commemoration of the Holy Great-Martyr Nicetas (d. ca. 372), a Gothic soldier martyred under the pagan chieftain Athanaric, whose memory is observed on the day following the Universal Exaltation (September 15).

[^1217]: p. 184 (Fol. 91v). First Prosomoion of the Precious Cross at 'Lord, I have cried' in Tone 5, Podoben 'Rejoice, life-bearing Cross'.

[^1218]: p. 184 (Fol. 91v). Second Prosomoion of the Precious Cross in Tone 5.

[^1219]: p. 184 (Fol. 91v). Third Prosomoion of the Precious Cross in Tone 5.

[^1220]: p. 184 (Fol. 91v). First Sticheron of St. Nicetas in Tone 4, Podoben 'As one valiant', referencing Gideon's victory in Judges 7.

[^1221]: p. 184 (Fol. 91v). Second Sticheron of St. Nicetas in Tone 4, commemorating his enduring of tortures and preaching among the Goths.

[^1222]: p. 185 (Fol. 92r). Third Sticheron of St. Nicetas in Tone 4, referencing the martyr offering the firstfruits of faith among the barbarian nations.

[^1223]: p. 185 (Fol. 92r). Doxastikon (Glory) of St. Nicetas at 'Lord, I have cried' in Tone 6, attributed in the rubric to St. Theophanes the Branded (ca. 778–845).

[^1224]: p. 185 (Fol. 92r). Festal Doxastikon (Both now) of the Exaltation in Tone 6.

[^1225]: p. 185 (Fol. 92r). Great Prokeimenon at Vespers from Psalm 113:11, 1-3, 5 (LXX).

[^1226]: p. 185 (Fol. 92r). First Aposticha Sticheron of the Precious Cross in Tone 6, Podoben 'Having laid aside all'.

[^1227]: p. 186 (Fol. 92v). Second Aposticha Sticheron of the Cross in Tone 6, celebrating the victory prefigured by Moses' outstretched hands against Amalek (Ex. 17:11).

[^1228]: p. 186 (Fol. 92v). Third Aposticha Sticheron of the Cross in Tone 6.

[^1229]: p. 186 (Fol. 92v). An epigraphic and etymological wordplay on the name Nicetas: Church Slavonic 'Побѣ́ды тезоимени́тъ' ('named after victory'), deriving from Greek Νικήτας (from νίκη, victory).

[^1230]: p. 186 (Fol. 92v). Doxastikon of St. Nicetas at the Aposticha in Tone 8.

[^1231]: p. 186 (Fol. 92v). Festal Both now at the Aposticha in Tone 8, citing Deuteronomy 28:66 and Psalm 73:12 (LXX).

[^1232]: p. 186 (Fol. 92v). Common Dismissal Troparion of a Martyr in Tone 4 ('Thy martyr, O Lord, Nicetas...').

[^1233]: p. 187 (Fol. 93r). Concluding phrase of the second Dismissal Troparion of the Holy Great-Martyr Nicetas.

[^1234]: p. 187 (Fol. 93r). First Kathisma Sessional Hymn of the Exaltation in Tone 2, Podoben 'Being compassionate'.

[^1235]: p. 187 (Fol. 93r). Second Kathisma Sessional Hymn of the Exaltation in Tone 3, Podoben 'At the beauty of thy virginity', meditating on the darkening of the sun (Luke 23:44-45) and the repentance of the Good Thief (Luke 23:42-43).

[^1236]: p. 187 (Fol. 93r). Festal Canon of the Exaltation of the Cross in Tone 8, composed by St. Cosmas the Melodist. Ode 1 Heirmos, interpreting Moses parting and closing the Red Sea (Ex. 14:21-28) crosswise with his staff.
